Acknowledgements

This material is based upon work supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research under award number FA9550-21-1-0024.
This work was partially funded by EPSRC grant EP/T000252/1 and by K&A Wallenberg Foundation grant 2019.0494.

2
Preliminaries: stratification of categories

In this section we collect definitions and results related to
stratification of strict categories

and morphisms between them.
A stratification (see
2.1
) associates, to any object of a strict category a natural number, its “length”,
and to any length-decreasing morphism a factorization of this morphism into morphisms “of length 1”.
Such a stratification can equivalently be described as a rooted tree, see
Section

2.2
.

2.1
Stratification of strict categories

Definition 2.1

(Stratified strict categories, stratified functors)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a strict category with terminal object 1.
A
stratification
for
\mathcal{C}
consists of a

stratification functor

L:\mathcal{C}\to(\mathbb{N},\geq)

such that

1.

L(X)=0
if and only if
X
is the chosen terminal object 1,

2.

for any
f:X\to Y
we have

L(X)=L(Y)
if and only if
X=Y
and
f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and

3.

every morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, where
L(X)=n+m+1
and
L(Y)=n
, has a unique factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X=X_{m+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}=Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

where
L(X_{i})=n+i
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between strict categories with stratifications
L_{\mathcal{C}}
and
L_{\mathcal{D}}
, respectively, is said to be
stratified

if
L_{\mathcal{C}}=L_{\mathcal{D}}\circ F
.

Remark 2.2
.

We emphasize that stratifications do not transport along equivalence of categories.
For instance, there is a (necessarily unique, see
2.8
) stratification on the terminal strict category, but no stratification on the strict chaotic category with two objects and a choice of terminal object.

Remark 2.3
.

Those readers familiar with Conduché functors might note that a stratified category
(\mathcal{C},L)
is equivalently a Conduché functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{N}
with discrete fibers which takes the chosen terminal object of
\mathcal{C}
to
0
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with a terminal object and
\ell\colon\mathrm{Ob}{\left(C\right)}\to\mathbb{N}
a function.
An arrow
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is
individual

if
\ell(X)=\ell(Y)+1
.

Remark 2.5
.

1.

In a stratified category, there is a unique terminal object 1.
More generally, if there is an arrow
1\to X
, then
X=1
.

2.

The factorisation of an arrow
f\colon X\to Y
such that
L(X)-L(Y)=m+1>0

in
2.1
.
3

consists of
m+1
individual arrows.

3.

A stratified functor is determined by its action on individual arrows.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with a terminal object
1
.
A function
\ell\colon\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}\to\mathbb{N}
lifts to a stratification

L\colon\mathcal{C}\to(\mathbb{N},\geq)
of
\mathcal{C}
if and only if the following three conditions hold:

(
i
)

\ell(1)=0
,

(
ii
)

for every object
X
and
k\leq\ell(X)
, the set

\coprod_{Y\mathop{\mid}\ell(Y)=k}\mathcal{C}(X,Y)

is a singleton, i.e. there is a unique arrow
x_{k}\colon X\to X_{k}

such that
\ell(X_{k})=k
, and

(
iii
)

for every
X
and
k>\ell(X)
, the set

\coprod_{Y\mathop{\mid}\ell(Y)=k}\mathcal{C}(X,Y)

is empty, i.e. there are no arrows
X\to Y
such that
\ell(X)<\ell(Y)
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{C}
is stratified by
L
such that
L(X)=\ell(X)
,
condition (
i
) follows from
2.1
.
1
.
To show condition (
ii
), note that every arrow
X\to 1
factors uniquely into
l=\ell(X)
individual arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{l-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{l-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}

In particular, for every
n\leq\ell(X)
, the composite
x_{n+1}\cdots x_{l}\colon X\to X_{n}
is such that
\ell(X_{n})=n
.
If
f\colon X\to Y
is also such that
\ell(Y)=n
, then
f
factors into
l-n
individual arrows
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{l-n}
.
Let
y_{1}\cdots y_{n}
be the factorisation of
Y\to 1
into individual arrows.
The composite
y_{1}\cdots y_{n}f_{0}\cdots f_{l-n-1}
is a factorisation of
X\to 1
into individual arrows.
It follows by uniqueness of such factorisations that

x_{1}=y_{1},\ x_{2}=y_{2},\ \dots\ ,\ x_{n}=y_{n},\ x_{n+1}=f_{1},\ \dots\ ,\ x_{l}=f_{l-n}.

In particular,
f=x_{n+1}\cdots x_{l}
as required.

Since
(\mathbb{N},\geq)
is a poset, condition (
iii
) is equivalent to the fact that
the function
\ell
extends uniquely to a functor
L\colon\mathcal{C}\to(\mathbb{N},\geq)
.

Suppose now that conditions (
i
–
iii
) above hold.
In particular, the function
\ell
extends to a functor
L
.

(
1
)
Let
X
be such that
L(X)=0
.
Then
X\to 1
and
\mathrm{id}_{X}\colon X\to X
are both such that
\ell(X)=0=\ell(1)
.
Hence
X=1
and the object
X
is terminal.
Conversely, let
X
be terminal. Then there is
1\to X
and thus
0\geq L(X)
.

(
2
)
Let
f\colon X\to Y
and suppose
L(X)=L(Y)
,
then
Y=X
and
f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
by (
ii
) with
n=\ell(X)
.

(
3
)
For every
X
such that
n+1=\ell(X)>0
, let
\overline{X}\colon X\to X^{\prime}

be the unique arrow such that
\ell(X^{\prime})=n
given by (
ii
).
For every
k\leq\ell(X)
, we have a composite
x_{k}
of
k
individual arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(k-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X^{(k-2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X^{(k-1)}}}

(3)

where
\ell(X^{(k)})=\ell(X)-k
,
which is the unique arrow
X\to Y
such that
\ell(Y)=\ell(X)-k
by (
ii
).
Let us show that (
3
) is also
the unique factorisation of
x_{k}
into individual arrows,
for every
0<k\leq\ell(X)
.
We proceed by induction on
n
.
If
n=0
, then factorisations consist of only one individual arrow
and uniqueness follow from (
ii
).
For
n>0
, let
0<k\leq n+1
and consider a factorisation

X\xrightarrow{g_{0}}Z_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{g_{k-2}}Z_{k-1}\xrightarrow{g_{k-1}}X^{(k)}

of
x_{k}
into individual arrows.
Then
\ell(Z_{1})=\ell(X)-1=\ell(X^{\prime})
, and so
g_{0}=\overline{X}
by (
ii
).
Again,
g_{k-1}\cdots g_{1}=x^{\prime}_{k-1}\colon X^{\prime}\to(X^{\prime})^{(k-1)}
by (
ii
) and,
by inductive hypothesis,
g_{i}=\overline{X^{(i)}}
for
0<i<k
.
Therefore (
3
) is the unique factorisation of
x_{k}

into individual arrows.

Given an arrow
f\colon X\to Y
such that
n=\ell(Y)<\ell(X)=m+n+1
,
it must be
Y=X^{(m+1)}
and
f=x_{m+1}
by (
ii
).
It follows that
f
factors uniquely into individuals as

\overline{X^{(m)}}\cdots\overline{X^{\prime}}\,\overline{X}
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Condition (
ii
) in
2.6
is equivalent to requiring that,
for every object
X
:

(
lem:strat-alt:down.a
)

for every
n\leq\ell(X)
there is at most one arrow
f\colon X\to Y
such that
\ell(Y)=n
, and

(
lem:strat-alt:down.b
)

there is an individual arrow
\overline{X}\colon X\to X^{\prime}
.

One direction is clear. For the converse it is enough to show that
for every
n<\ell(X)
there is
f\colon X\to Y
such that
\ell(Y)=n
.
Such an arrow is given as the composite of
\ell(X)-n
individual arrows
as in
Eq.

3
above.

Proposition 2.8
.

Any category can be stratified in at most one way.

Proof.

Consider a category
\mathcal{C}
with two stratifications
L,M:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{N}
.
By
2.6
.
ii
,

L^{-1}(n+1)=\left\{X\mid\exists\ Y\in L^{-1}(n)\text{ and an individual }f:X\to Y\right\}

and similarly for
M^{-1}(n+1)
.
Thus, if
L^{-1}(n)=M^{-1}(n)
, we find that
L^{-1}(n+1)=M^{-1}(n+1)
,
and the claim follows by induction
since
L^{-1}(0)=M^{-1}(0)
by
2.1
.
1
.
∎

Uniqueness of stratification justifies the following definition:

Definition 2.9
.

We define
\mathbf{Cat_{s}}
to be the subcategory of
\mathbf{Cat}
consisting of stratified strict categories and stratified functors between them.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor between stratified categories.
The following are equivalent.

1.

The functor
F
is stratified.

2.

The functor
F
preserves terminal objects and individual arrows.

Proof.

That
1
implies

2
is clear.
The converse is by induction on the length of objects using that,
for every
f\colon X\to Y
,

L_{\mathcal{C}}(X)=L_{\mathcal{C}}(Y)+1
implies
L_{\mathcal{D}}(F(X))=L_{\mathcal{D}}(F(Y))+1
.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stratified category with stratification functor
L
.
Then for every object
X
and every
f\colon Y\to X
,

L_{X}(f):=L(Y)-L(X)

defines a stratification functor
L_{X}
for the slice
\mathcal{C}/X
.

Proof.

The above clearly defines a functor
L_{X}\colon\mathcal{C}/X\to(\mathbb{N},\geq)
and
conditions (
1
–
3
) in
2.1
are easily verified.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a stratified functor
between stratified categories.
For every object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}/X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}/FX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/X}

is stratified.

2.2
Rooted trees

In this section, we compare stratified categories to rooted trees.
Rooted trees were used by Cartmell
[
Car86
]
to give his original definition of contextual categories.

Definition 2.13
.

1.

We define a
rooted tree

T
to be a family of sets

(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
indexed by the natural numbers
such that
T_{0}
is a singleton, together with functions

(t_{n}\colon T_{n+1}\to T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

mapping a node to its parent.
A homomorphism of rooted trees
f\colon T\to S
is a family of functions

(f_{n}\colon T_{n}\to S_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

such that
f_{n}\circ t_{n}=s_{n}\circ f_{n+1}

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{RtTr}
be the category of rooted trees and homomorphisms.

2.

Let
\mathbf{Grph}
be the category of directed (multi)graphs and homomorphisms.
We define the functor
\mathbf{G}:\mathbf{RtTr}\to\mathbf{Grph}
as follows.
For a rooted tree
T
, the directed graph
\mathbf{G}(T)

has the set of vertices given by the disjoint union
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}T_{n}
,
and there is an edge
(n+1,X)\to(n,t_{n}(X))

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in T_{n+1}
.
It is straightforward to verify that each homomorphism
T\to T^{\prime}
of rooted trees
gives rise to a homomorphism of graphs
\mathbf{G}(T)\to\mathbf{G}(T^{\prime})
.

3.

Let
\mathbf{F}:\mathbf{Grph}\to\mathbf{Cat}
be the well-known functor
[
Mac98
, II.7]

that takes a graph to the category freely generated by it.

We now show that
the image of the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{RtTr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Grph}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}}

is the subcategory of
stratified strict categories
defined in
2.9
.

Proposition 2.14
.

The functor
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}\colon\mathbf{RtTr}\to\mathbf{Cat}
lifts to an equivalence
\mathbf{RtTr}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Cat_{s}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that, for a rooted tree
T
, the free category
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}(T)
is stratified.
We define
L:\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}(T)\to(\mathbb{N},\geq)
by sending an object
(n,X)
to
n
and a generating morphism
(n+1,X)\to(n,t_{n}(X))
to
n+1\geq n
.
Given a morphism
f:S\to T
of rooted trees, the functor
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}(f):\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}(S)\to\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}(T)
is stratified by construction.
Thus, the functor
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}:\mathbf{RtTr}\to\mathbf{Cat}
lifts to an functor
\mathbf{RtTr}\to\mathbf{Cat_{s}}
.

Next we define a functor
\mathbf{I}\colon\mathbf{Cat_{s}}\to\mathbf{RtTr}
.
Consider a stratified category
(\mathcal{C},L)
and define a rooted tree
\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},L)
as follows. Let
\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},L)_{n}:=L^{-1}(n)
.
By
2.6
, for every
X\in\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},L)_{n}
there is
exactly one individual arrow, say
X\to X^{\prime}
.
Then we define
t_{n}:\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},L)_{n+1}\to\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},L)_{n}
by
t_{n}(X):=X^{\prime}
.
A stratified functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces
a homomorphism of rooted trees
\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},L)\to\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{C},M)

since it commutes with the stratification functors
and it preserves individual arrows.

It is now straightforward to verify that

\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{I}\cong 1_{\mathbf{Cat_{s}}}
and

\mathbf{I}\circ\mathbf{F}\mathbf{G}\cong 1_{\mathbf{RtTr}}
.
∎

3
The category of C-systems

This section is dedicated to the study of C-systems.

In
Section

3.1
we review Voevodsky’s definition of C-system, itself a mild variant of Cartmell’s contextual categories.
We then give, in
Section

3.2
our definition of CE-system, and identify,
in
Section

3.3
, the category of C-systems as a subcategory of “stratified” objects in the category of CE-systems.

3.1
The category of C-systems

John Cartmell
[
Car86
, Section 14]
defined
contextual categories
as mathematical gadgets for the interpretation of type theories.
Vladimir Voevodsky
[
Voe16b
, Definition 2.1]
gave a slightly modified, but obviously equivalent definition, and coined them
C-systems
.

Definition 3.1

(C-system,
[
Voe16b
, Def. 2.1]
)
.

A
C-system
consists of

1.

a strict category
\mathcal{C}
,

2.

a “length” function
{\ell}:\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}\to\mathbb{N}
,

3.

a chosen object
\mathsf{1}\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
,

4.

a function
\mathsf{ft}:\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}\to\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
,

5.

for any object
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
such that
{\ell}(\Gamma)>0
, a morphism
\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
,

6.

for any
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
with
{\ell}(\Gamma)>0
and any
f:\Delta\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
, an object
f^{*}\Gamma
and a morphism
\mathsf{q}(f,\Gamma):f^{*}\Gamma\to\Gamma
.

satisfying the following axioms:

i
)

{\ell}^{-1}(0)=\{\mathsf{1}\}
,

ii
)

for
\Gamma
with
{\ell}(\Gamma)>0
, we have
{\ell}(\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma))={\ell}(\Gamma)-1
,

iii
)

\mathsf{ft}(\mathsf{1})=\mathsf{1}
,

iv
)

\mathsf{1}
is a final object,

v
)

for
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
with
{\ell}(\Gamma)>0
and
f:\Delta\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
,
one has
{\ell}(f^{*}\Gamma)>0
,
\mathsf{ft}(f^{*}\Gamma)=\Delta
, and the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{q}(f,\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{f^{*}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(4)

commutes and is a pullback square,

vi
)

for
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
with
{\ell}(\Gamma)>0
one has
\left(\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)}\right)^{*}\Gamma=\Gamma
and
\mathsf{q}(\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)},\Gamma)=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}
, and

vii
)

for
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
with
{\ell}(\Gamma)>0
,
g:\Delta\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
and
f:E\to\Delta
, we have
(g\circ f)^{*}\Gamma=f^{*}g^{*}\Gamma
and
\mathsf{q}(g\circ f,\Gamma)=\mathsf{q}(g,\Gamma)\circ\mathsf{q}(f,g^{*}\Gamma)
.

Intuitively, an object
\Gamma
of the category underlying a C-system can be thought of as a context of length
\ell(\Gamma)
.
Types do not appear explicitly in the definition of C-system;
however, intuitively, the types in context
\Gamma
are all those contexts
\Delta
with
\mathsf{ft}(\Delta)=\Gamma
(hence, in particular,
{\ell}(\Delta)={\ell}(\Gamma)+1
).
Similarly, terms are not explicitly given; a term in context
\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
corresponds to a section to
\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}\Gamma\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
.
This is exactly how terms are defined in the definition of an E-system from a CE-system, see
5.6
.

The length function
\ell
does not lift in general
to a stratification on
\mathcal{C}
.
However, it does on a suitable subcategory, see
3.9
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
morphism of C-systems
from
\mathbb{C}
to
\mathbb{D}
is a functor

F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between the underlying categories that strictly preserves the rest of the structure, that is:

i
)

F(\mathsf{1}_{\mathbb{C}})=\mathsf{1}_{\mathbb{D}}
,

ii
)

\ell_{\mathbb{D}}\circ\mathrm{Ob}{\left(F\right)}=\ell_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}\to\mathbb{N}
,

iii
)

\mathrm{Ob}{\left(F\right)}\circ\mathsf{ft}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathsf{ft}_{\mathbb{D}}\circ\mathrm{Ob}{\left(F\right)}\colon\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}\to\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{D}\right)}
,

iv
)

F\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}=\mathsf{p}_{F\Gamma}
, for every
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
,

v
)

F(f^{*}\Gamma)=(Ff)^{*}(F\Gamma)
and
F(\mathsf{q}(f,\Gamma))=\mathsf{q}(Ff,F\Gamma)
,
for every
\Gamma\in\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
such that
\ell_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)>0
and
f\colon\Delta\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
.

Example 3.3

(C-systems and Lavwere theories)
.

Fiore and Voevodsky
[
FV20
]
construct an isomorphism of categories between the category of Lawvere theories and the category of
\ell
-bijective
C-systems, that is, of C-systems whose length function is a bijection. Intuitively, such a C-system can be seen as modelling an untyped (or single-sorted) language.

Example 3.4

(C-systems and contextual categories)
.

C-systems are equivalent to Cartmell’s contextual categories.
In his Ph.D. dissertation, Cartmell
[
Car78
, Section 2.4]
constructs an equivalence between the category of contextual categories and homomorphisms between them and the category of Generalized Algebraic Theories (GATs) and (equivalence classes of) interpretations between them.
Hence C-systems are equivalent to GATs.

Example 3.5

(C-system from a universe category)
.

Any universe category gives rise to a C-system, via a construction by Voevodsky
[
Voe15a
, Construction 2.12]
.
A universe category is a category with a chosen terminal object and a
universe
, that is, a morphism
p:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{U}
together with a choice of pullback of
p
along any morphism
X\to\mathcal{U}
.
Roughly, the C-system constructed from a universe category has, as objects of length
n
, sequences of
n
morphisms
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
into
\mathcal{U}
such that the domain of
f_{i+1}
is the chosen pullback of
p
along
f_{i}
. Such a sequence can be thought of as a sequence of (dependent) types
(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{n})
such that
A_{1},\ldots A_{i}\vdash A_{i+1}
.
Furthermore, any small C-system can be obtained via this construction;
given a C-system
\mathbb{C}
, a universe can be constructed (
[
Voe15a
, Construction 5.2]
) on the presheaf category
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
such that the C-system obtained from that universe is isomorphic to the C-system
\mathbb{C}
.
For a brief overview of these constructions, see
[
KL21
, Section 1.3]
.

Voevodsky’s simplicial model of univalent foundations
[
KL21
]
is built on top of a C-system obtained from a universe in the category of simplicial sets.

Problem 3.6
.

To construct a functor
\mathbf{C2RtTr}\colon\mathbf{Csys}\to\mathbf{RtTr}
.

Construction 3.7

(for
3.6
)
.

Let
\mathbb{C}=(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{1},\ell,\mathsf{ft},\mathsf{p},\dots)
be a C-system.
The objects of
\mathcal{C}
can be arranged into a rooted tree by defining

T_{n}:=\left\{\Gamma\mid\ell(\Gamma)=n\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad t_{n}(\Gamma):=\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)\in T_{n},\text{ for }\Gamma\in T_{n+1}.

That is, the front square in the diagram of sets and functions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pred}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

is a pullback for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
and the function
t_{n}
is defined by its universal property
as the right-hand square commutes by
3.1
.
ii
.
The set
T_{0}
is a singleton by (
i
) in
3.1
.

A homomorphism of C-systems
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
restricts, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
to a function
F_{n}:T_{n}\to S_{n}

between the fibres
T_{n}
and
S_{n}
of the length function of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively,
by
3.2
.
ii

as in the front part of the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{D}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(\mathcal{D}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(F\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ob}{\left(F\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pred}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

(5)

The upper-right square commutes by
3.2
.
iii
,
thus the upper-left square commutes as well since the rest of the diagram commutes.
Functoriality holds since each
F_{n}
is defined by a universal property.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a C-system with underlying strict category
\mathcal{C}
and
let
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
denote the wide subgraph of
\mathcal{C}
on the canonical projections

\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}
for
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
is isomorphic to the graph
\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{C2RtTr}(\mathbb{C})

naturally in
\mathbb{C}
,
where
\mathbf{G}\colon\mathbf{RtTr}\to\mathbf{Grph}
is from
2.13
.

Proof.

The vertices of
\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{C2RtTr}(\mathbb{C})
are pairs
(\ell(\Gamma),\Gamma)

and edges are of the form

(\ell(\Gamma),\Gamma)\to(\ell(\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)),\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma))

for
\ell(\Gamma)>0
.
In particular, every vertex
(n+1,\Gamma)

has exactly one outgoing edge.
The bijection between vertices then extends to an isomorphism
between
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{C2RtTr}(\mathbb{C})
.

Every C-homomorphism
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
induces
a morphism of graphs
\mathsf{p}(F)\colon\mathsf{p}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathsf{p}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}})

by
3.2
.
iv
.
Naturality then follows from
3.2
ii
.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the free category on the graph
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
on the canonical projections.
Then the terminal object
\mathsf{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
is terminal in
\mathcal{F}
and
the function
\ell
extends to a stratification functor on
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

By
3.8
there is an iso

\mathcal{F}=\mathbf{F}\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbf{F}\circ\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{C2RtTr}(\mathbb{C})
.
The claim thus follows from
2.14
.
∎

3.2
The category of CE-systems

In this section, we define CE-systems and their morphisms.

Definition 3.10
.

A
CE-system
consists of
two strict category structures
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{C}

on the same set of objects
\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{F})=\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})

and an identity-on-objects functor
I\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
between them,
together with

1.

a chosen object
\mathsf{1}
which is terminal in
\mathcal{F}
, and

2.

for any
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}
and any
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
,
a functorial choice of a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{{f}^{*}\!{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma}.{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(f,A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I({f}^{*}\!{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that
{f}^{*}\!{A}\in\mathcal{F}/\Delta
.
Explicitly, the functoriality requirement is that

(a)

For any
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
, one has

{f}^{*}\!{\left(\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}\right)}=\mathrm{id}_{\Delta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{2}\left(f,\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}\right)=f.

(b)

For any
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
, one has

{\left(\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}\right)}^{*}\!{A}=A\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{2}\left(\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma},A\right)=\mathrm{id}_{{\Gamma}.{A}}

(c)

For any
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
,
g\colon\Xi\to\Delta
and
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
, one has

{\left(f\circ g\right)}^{*}\!{A}={g}^{*}\!{\left({f}^{*}\!{A}\right)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{2}\left(f\circ g,A\right)=\pi_{2}\left(f,A\right)\circ\pi_{2}\left(g,{f}^{*}\!{A}\right)

(d)

For any
P\in\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}
and
f:\Delta\to\Gamma
, one has

{f}^{*}\!{\left({A}.{P}\right)}={f}^{*}\!{A}\circ{\left(\pi_{2}\left(f,A\right)\right)}^{*}\!{P}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi_{2}\left(f,{A}.{P}\right)=\pi_{2}\left(\pi_{2}\left(f,A\right),P\right)

A CE-system is
rooted

if
I(\mathsf{1})=\mathsf{1}
is terminal in
\mathcal{C}
.

For any
f:\Delta\to\Gamma
we write
{f}^{*}\!{\,}
for the induced functor

\mathcal{F}/\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}/\Delta

and refer to the arrows in
\mathcal{F}
as the
families
of the CE-system.
We shall write arrows in
\mathcal{F}
with a double head as in the above diagram.

We may write
I_{{\mathbb{A}}}\colon\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{A}}}\to\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{A}}}
for the functor
underlying a CE-system
\mathbb{A}
,
whenever we need to make the CE-system explicit.

We show in
Section

3.3
that CE-systems generalize C-systems.
To provide some intuition, we can think of
\mathcal{F}
as the category spanned by the projections
\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\to\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)
of a C-system; in this case, the functor
I\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
is faithful.

We now give two examples of CE-system.

Example 3.11

(CE-system on finite sets)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be the category whose objects are natural numbers, and whose morphisms
f:m\to n

are functions
f:\mathsf{std}(m)\to\mathsf{std}(n)
from the standard finite set of
m
elements to the standard finite set of
n
elements.
Consider the identity-on-objects functor
[-]:(\mathbb{N},\geq)\to\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}
given, on
n+k\geq n
, by the opposite of the initial-segment inclusion, which we write
i_{n}^{n+k}:[n+k]\to[n]
.

We equip it with the structure of a CE-system as follows.
The chosen pullback of a family
n+k\geq n
and an arrow
f\colon[m]\to[n]
in
\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}
is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[m+k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[n+k]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m}^{m+k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(f,n+k\geq n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}^{n+k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where the morphism
\pi_{2}(f,n+k\geq n)
is the opposite of the arrow
[f,1_{k}]\colon[n+k]\to[m+k]
in
\mathbb{F}

obtained from the universal property of the coproduct
[n+k]
.
Functoriality follows immediately from the definitions.

This CE-system is, of course, rooted —
as
[0]
is terminal in
\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{op}}
—
and stratified in the sense of
3.15
—
as initial-segment inclusions factor uniquely into individuals
i_{n}^{n+1}
.
Note also that the choice of pullback squares is forced by
3.18
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be two CE-systems.
A
CE-homomorphism

F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}

consists of a commutative square of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathcal{C}}}

such that,

1.

F_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathsf{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}})=\mathsf{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}
, and

2.

for every
A\in\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{A}}}/\Gamma
and
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
,
it is

F_{\mathcal{F}}({f}^{*}\!{A})={\left(F_{\mathcal{C}}f\right)}^{*}\!{\left(F_{\mathcal{F}}A\right)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{\mathcal{C}}(\pi_{2}\left(f,A\right))=\pi_{2}\left(F_{\mathcal{C}}f,F_{\mathcal{F}}A\right).

Remark 3.13
.

If
F
is a CE-homomorphism between rooted CE-systems
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
,
then
F_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}})=\mathsf{1}_{{\mathbb{B}}}
and

F_{\mathcal{C}}
preserves terminal objects in the usual categorical sense.

Definition 3.14
.

We write
\mathbf{CEsys}
for the category of CE-systems and CE-system homomorphisms
and
\mathbf{rCEsys}
for its full subcategory on rooted CE-systems.

For the comparison of CE-systems with C-systems, the notion of stratification of a CE-system is needed:

Definition 3.15
.

A CE-system
\mathbb{A}
is
stratified
if its category of families
\mathcal{F}

is stratified in the sense of
2.1
and,
for every
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}
, the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

induced by the functorial choice of pullbacks is stratified
with respect to the stratification induced on slices in
2.11
.

A CE-homomorphism between stratified CE-systems is
stratified

if its component on families is a stratified functor.

Remark 3.16
.

It follows from
2.8
that CE-systems are stratified in at most one way.

Definition 3.17
.

We denote by
\mathbf{\mathbf{CEsys}_{s}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CEsys}
and
\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{rCEsys}

the respective subcategories spanned by
stratified
(rooted) CE-systems and
stratified
CE-homomorphisms between them.

Remark 3.18
.

In a stratified CE-system,
for every
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}
and
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
it is

L(\Delta.f^{*}A)=L(\Delta)+L(\Gamma.A)-L(\Gamma).

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
be two stratified CE-system.
A commuting square of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathcal{C}}}

is a stratified CE-homomorphism
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
if and only if

1.

F_{\mathcal{F}}
is a stratified functor, and

2.

for every individual arrow
A\in\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{A}}}/\Gamma

and every
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
,
it is

F_{\mathcal{F}}({f}^{*}\!{A})={\left(F_{\mathcal{C}}f\right)}^{*}\!{\left(F_{\mathcal{F}}A\right)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{\mathcal{C}}(\pi_{2}\left(f,A\right))=\pi_{2}\left(F_{\mathcal{C}}f,F_{\mathcal{F}}A\right).

Proof.

One direction is trivial.
The other one is proved by induction on the length
n
of an arrow
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
.
∎

3.3
Characterising C-systems as stratified CE-systems

Recall from
3.9
that
every C-system
\mathbb{C}
has a stratified wide subcategory
\mathcal{F}
of its underlying category
\mathcal{C}
.
In this section, we show that the inclusion
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}

has the structure of a stratified CE-system (
3.21
).
Moreover, we prove that this correspondence is functorial (
3.24
)
and, in fact, an equivalence between the category of C-systems and the category of stratified CE-systems (
3.30
).

Problem 3.20
.

To construct a CE-system
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})

from a C-system
\mathbb{C}=(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{1},{\ell},\mathsf{ft},\ldots)
.

Construction 3.21

(for
3.20
)
.

Recall from
3.8
that

\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
denotes the wide subgraph of
\mathcal{C}
on the canonical projections

\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}
for
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}

and let
\mathcal{F}
be the free category on
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
.
In particular,
\mathcal{F}
has the same objects of
\mathcal{C}

and the object
\mathsf{1}
is terminal in
\mathcal{F}
by
3.9
.
It follows that the inclusion
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}

extends to an identity-on-objects functor
I\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}

that maps a path of length
n>0

in
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
,
i.e.
a list of composable canonical projections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}^{n-1}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}^{n}(\Gamma).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{\mathsf{ft}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{\mathsf{ft}^{2}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{\mathsf{ft}^{n-1}(\Gamma)}}

(6)

to their composite in
\mathcal{C}
.

It remains to provide
I
with a suitable choice of pullback squares
along an arbitrary arrow
f\colon\Delta\to\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}
.
As an arrow
p\colon\Xi\to\Gamma
in
\mathcal{F}
is a path in
\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})
,
we proceed by induction on the length
n
of the path
p
,
proving also conditions (
2b
) and (
2c
)
from
3.10
.

If
n=0
, the path
p
is the identity on
\Gamma
and we take

f^{*}(\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}):=\mathrm{id}_{\Delta}
and
\pi_{2}(f,\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}):=f
.
This choice is clearly functorial in
f

and it trivially gives rise to a pullback square.
It also ensures condition (
2a
).

For
n>0
, it is
I(p)=\mathsf{p}_{\Xi}\circ I(p^{\prime})
where the length of
p^{\prime}
is
n-1
.
By inductive hypothesis we have
f^{*}p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}/\Delta
and a chosen pullback square of
I(p^{\prime})
along
f
,
which is the lower square in the diagram below.
The upper square is the canonical pullback square (
4
) given by the C-system structure.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}))^{*}\Xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{f^{*}(p^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}(\Xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}))^{*}\Xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{q}(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}),\Xi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{\Xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(f^{*}p^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(p^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(7)

Thus we define
\pi_{2}(f,p):=\mathsf{q}(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}),\Xi)
and

f^{*}p
to be the concatenation of
f^{*}p^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{p}_{\mathsf{p}_{(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}))^{*}\Xi}}

so that
I(f^{*}p)=I(f^{*}p^{\prime})\circ\mathsf{p}_{\mathsf{p}_{(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}))^{*}\Xi}}
.
Functoriality in
f
of this choice of pullback squares follows
from the fact that both the lower and upper pullback squares are functorial
by inductive hypothesis and by assumption, respectively.
In more details:
given
g\colon\Theta\to\Delta
, the inductive hypothesis yields

(f\circ g)^{*}p^{\prime}=g^{*}(f^{*}p^{\prime})
and

\pi_{2}(f\circ g,p^{\prime})=\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime})\circ\pi_{2}(g,f^{*}p^{\prime})
.
It follows by
3.1
.
vii
that

\pi_{2}(f\circ g,p^{\prime})^{*}\Xi=\pi_{2}(g,f^{*}p^{\prime})^{*}{\left(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime})^{*}\Xi\right)}

and, in turn, that
(f\circ g)^{*}p=g^{*}(f^{*}p)
.
The other component also follows from
3.1
.
vii
:

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(f\circ g,p)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}(\pi_{2}(f\circ g,p^{\prime}),\Xi)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}(\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime}),\Xi)\circ\mathsf{q}(\pi_{2}(g,f^{*}p^{\prime}),\pi_{2}(f,p^{\prime})^{*}{\Xi})

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(f,p)\circ\pi_{2}(g,f^{*}p).

Finally, condition (
2d
) for a composite
q\circ p
in
\mathcal{F}

is proven by induction on the length of the path
p
.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a C-system and
\mathcal{F}
the category of families of
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})
.

1.

The individual arrows in
\mathcal{F}
are of the form
\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}

for some object
\Gamma
.

2.

The CE-system
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})
is stratified and

L(\Gamma)=\ell(\Gamma)
, for every object
\Gamma
.

3.

The CE-system
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})
is rooted.

Proof.

1
.

Immediate from the description of arrows in
\mathcal{F}
in (
6
)
and
3.1
.
ii
.

2
.

By
3.9
,
the category
\mathcal{F}
is stratified
and
L(\Gamma)=\ell(\Gamma)
.
By
2.10
, it is enough to show that the choice of pullback squares
in
3.21
preserves individuals.
But this follows immediately from the construction of pullbacks in (
7
)
and (
1
) just shown.

3
.

The terminal object in
\mathcal{F}
is terminal in
\mathcal{C}
by assumption. ∎

Problem 3.23
.

To construct a functor
\mathbf{CE}\colon\mathbf{Csys}\to\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}

into rooted stratified CE-systems and stratified homomorphisms.

Construction 3.24

(for
3.23
)
.

The action of
\mathbf{CE}
on objects is defined in
3.21
.
Every morphism
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
of C-systems
restricts to the graphs of canonical projections

\mathsf{p}(F)\colon\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{D})

by conditions (
i
,
iii
,
iv
)
in
3.2

and induces, in turn, a functor between free categories

F_{\mathcal{F}}\colon\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{D}}

whose action is determined by the action of
F
on individual arrows.
The square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

commutes since it does so when precomposed by the unit

\mathsf{p}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
The functor
F_{\mathcal{F}}
is stratified by
3.2
.
ii
.

3.19
then ensures that the pair
\mathbf{CE}(F):=(F_{\mathcal{F}},F)

lifts to a stratified CE-homomorphism as soon as
it preserves pullbacks of individual arrows.
But this is precisely condition
3.2
.
v
.
Functoriality of
\mathbf{CE}
follows since
F_{\mathcal{F}}
is defined by a universal property.
∎

Problem 3.25
.

To construct a C-system
\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A})
from a stratified and rooted CE-system
\mathbb{A}
.

Construction 3.26

(for
3.25
)
.

Let
I\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
be the underlying functor of
\mathbb{A}
.
The underlying category of
\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A})
is
\mathcal{C}

and the length function
\ell
is given by the action of the stratification functor

L
on objects.
Since
\mathbb{A}
is rooted, the chosen terminal object
\mathsf{1}
in
\mathcal{F}
is terminal in
\mathcal{C}
too.
Conditions (
iv
) and (
i
) are clearly met.

Given an object
X
with
n=L(X)>0
,
let
X\xrightarrow{x_{n}}X_{n-1}\to\cdots\to X_{1}\xrightarrow{x_{1}}\mathsf{1}

be the factorisation of
X\to\mathsf{1}
into
n
individuals in
\mathcal{F}
.
We define

\mathsf{ft}(\mathsf{1}):=\mathsf{1},\quad\mathsf{ft}(X):=X_{n-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{p}_{X}:=I(x_{n}).

(8)

Conditions (
ii
) and (
iii
)
hold by construction.

Given also
f\colon Y\to\mathsf{ft}(X)
,
let
Y\xrightarrow{y_{n}}Y_{n-1}\to\cdots\to Y_{1}\xrightarrow{y_{1}}\mathsf{1}

be the factorisation of
Y\to\mathsf{1}
into individuals and
consider the pullback square below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.f^{*}\!x_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(f^{*}\!x_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(f,x_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(x_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(9)

It is
L(Y.f^{*}(x_{n}))=L(Y)+1

by
3.18
, thus

Y.f^{*}x_{n}\xrightarrow{f^{*}\!x_{n}}Y\xrightarrow{y_{n}}Y_{n-1}\to\cdots\to Y_{1}\xrightarrow{y_{1}}\mathsf{1}

is the factorisation of
Y.f^{*}\!x_{n}\to\mathsf{1}
into individuals.
It follows that
\mathsf{ft}(Y.f^{*}\!x_{n})=Y
and
\mathsf{p}_{f^{*}\!x_{n}}=I(f^{*}\!x_{n})
.
Condition (
v
) follows defining
f^{*}X:=Y.f^{*}(x_{n})
and
\mathsf{q}(f,X):=\pi_{2}(f,x_{n})
.
Condition (
vi
) holds by
3.10
.
2b
since
\mathsf{ft}(X).x_{n}=X
,
and (
vii
) by
3.10
.
2c
as below:

\displaystyle(f\circ g)^{*}X

\displaystyle=Z.(f\circ g)^{*}x_{n}=Z.{\left(g^{*}(f^{*}\!x_{n})\right)}

\displaystyle=g^{*}(f^{*}X)

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}(f\circ g,X)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(f\circ g,x_{n})=\pi_{2}(f,x_{n})\circ\pi_{2}(g,f^{*}\!x_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}(f,X)\circ\mathsf{q}(g,f^{*}X).\qed

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
be a stratified homomorphism
of rooted stratified CE-systems.
Then the underlying functor

F\colon\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{A}}}\to\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{B}}}

is a homomorphism of C-systems

\mathbf{C}(F)\colon\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{B})
.

Proof.

We verify the conditions in
3.2
.
(
i
)
The functor
F
maps the chosen terminal object of
\mathbb{A}

to the one of
\mathbb{B}
by assumption.
(
ii
)
Since
F
is stratified,
its action on objects commutes with the length functions.
(
iii
–
iv
)
The action on objects also
preserves individual arrows by
2.10
,
thus it commutes with the father functions
and preserves canonical projections.
(
v
)

F
maps chosen pullback squares in
\mathbb{A}

to chosen ones in
\mathbb{B}
by
3.12
.
2
.
In particular, it preserves the choice of pullbacks along individual arrows.
∎

Definition 3.28
.

Let
\mathbf{C}\colon\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}\to\mathbf{Csys}
be the functor given
by
3.26
and
3.27
.

Lemma 3.29
.

For every C-system
\mathbb{C}
,
the identity functor on the underlying strict category of
\mathbb{C}

is an isomorphism
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C}))\cong\mathbb{C}
of C-systems,
naturally in
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the underlying strict category of
\mathbb{C}
.
To see that the identity functor
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-homomorphism note first that
the category
\mathcal{C}
, its terminal object and the length function
are the same in
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C}))
and
\mathbb{C}
.
Since individual arrows in
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})

coincide with the canonical projections
\mathsf{p}_{\Gamma}

by
3.22
,
factorisations in
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})
into individual arrows
are of the form in (
6
).
It follows that the function
\mathsf{ft}
and the canonical projections
as defined in (
8
)
are equal to the ones from
\mathbb{C}
.
Since the choice of pullback squares in
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbb{C})

is defined inductively by the choice along individual arrows in (
7
),
the choice of pullbacks along canonical projections in (
9
)
coincides with the one in
\mathbb{C}
.

Naturality follows from the fact that
\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{CE}(F))=F

for every C-homomorphism
F
.
∎

Theorem 3.30
.

The functor
\mathbf{CE}\colon\mathbf{Csys}\to\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}

from
3.24

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By
3.29
, it is enough to find,
for every stratified rooted CE-system
\mathbb{A}
,
an isomorphism
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A}))\cong\mathbb{A}

natural in
\mathbb{A}
.
Let
I\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{C}
be the underlying functor of
\mathbb{A}

and let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{i}}:=\mathsf{p}(\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A}))

be the subgraph of
\mathcal{F}
on the individual arrows.
The CE-system
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A}))
consists, in particular,
of a functor
\widehat{I}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{F}}\to\mathcal{C}
,
where
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
is the free category on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{i}}
,
and
\widehat{I}
maps a list of composable individual arrows
to the composite of their images in
\mathcal{C}
under
I
,
by (
8
), (
6
) and
3.22
.
2
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{i}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
induces
an identity-on-objects functor
\mathsf{comp}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{F}}\to\mathcal{F}
.
Conversely, the factorisation into individual arrows (
3
)
in
\mathbb{A}
yields an identity-on-objects functor
\mathsf{fact}\colon\mathcal{F}\to\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
,
which is a (strict) inverse of
\mathsf{comp}
.
Since
I
is a functor, the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{comp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{fact}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}

(10)

commute.
Since both functors
\mathsf{comp}
and
\mathsf{fact}

are identities on objects and on individual arrows,
the squares above are stratified CE-homomorphisms

\mathbf{CE}(\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A}))\to\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}\to\mathbf{CE}(\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A}))
,
respectively, by
3.19
.
Therefore
\mathbf{CE}(\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{A}))\cong\mathbb{A}
.

To see that this isomorphism is natural in
\mathbb{A}
,
note that
(\mathsf{comp},\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})
is natural in
\mathbb{A}

since
\mathsf{comp}
is equivalently defined as the composite of
the counit of the free-forgetful adjunction at
\mathcal{F}

with the image under the left adjoint
of the graph inclusion
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{i}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
.
∎

4
The category of B-systems

In this section, we study Voevodsky’s B-systems.

In
Section

4.1
we review the definition of B-systems and their homomorphisms.
In
Section

4.2
we introduce the notion of E-system and their homomorphisms.
Intuitively, E-systems model type theory with strict
\Sigma
-types, see
Section

4.2.5
.
Finally, in
Section

4.3
we construct an equivalence between the category of B-systems and the subcategory of “stratified” E-systems.

In order to simplify the construction of such equivalence,
we structure the definitions in the next sections in three step.
In the case of B-systems, for example,
we first introduce some piece of structure on sets
consisting of functions,
which we refer to as
pre
-B-systems,
see
4.7
.
Then we define morphisms between these structures,
also called
pre
-homomorphisms,
and finally we define B-systems as those pre-B-systems
whose structure functions are themselves pre-homomorphisms.
Homomorphisms are then just pre-homomorphisms between B-systems.
We shall follow the same pattern
when introducing each of the structures that give rise to an E-system,
in
Sections

4.2.1
,
4.2.2
and
4.2.3
,
and when defining E-systems in
Section

4.2.4
.

4.1
The category of B-systems

In this section, we review the definition of Voevodsky’s B-systems
[
Voe14
]
.
We introduce a few auxiliary intermediate definitions
which we will use in later constructions.

Definition 4.1
.

A
B-frame

\mathbb{B}
is a diagram of sets of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\ast\}\cong B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ft}}

In other words, a B-frame consists of:

1.

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
two sets
B_{n}
and
\tilde{B}_{n+1}
.

2.

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
functions of the form

\displaystyle\mathsf{ft}_{n}

\displaystyle:B_{n+1}\to B_{n}

\displaystyle\partial_{n}

\displaystyle:\tilde{B}_{n+1}\to B_{n+1}.

3.

B_{0}
is a singleton.

For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote the composition
\mathsf{ft}_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\mathsf{ft}_{n+m}:B_{n+m+1}\to B_{n}
by
\mathsf{ft}_{n}^{m}
.

A
homomorphism
H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
of B-frames
is a natural transformation of B-frames, i.e., it consists of maps

H_{n}:B_{n}\to A_{n}
and
\tilde{H}_{n+1}:\tilde{B}_{n+1}\to\tilde{A}_{n+1}
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{ft}(H(X))

\displaystyle=H(\mathsf{ft}(X))

\displaystyle\partial(\tilde{H}(x))

\displaystyle=H(\partial(x))

for any
X\in B_{n}
and
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
. The category of B-frames is
denoted by
\mathbf{Bfr}
.

To provide some intuition for B-frames, we look back at the introduction, where we constructed, implicitly, a B-frame from a module over a monad.

Example 4.2
.

Recall from
Section

1.2.2
the two sets
B(R,LM)
(see
Eq.

1
) and
\widetilde{B}(R,LM)
(see
Eq.

2
).
From these sets, we obtain a B-frame with the following sets of families (note the shift in the indexing of
\tilde{B}
),

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle:=B(R,LM)_{n}:=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}LM({[i]})

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{B}(R,LM)_{n}:=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}LM({[i]})\times RR({[n]})\times LM({[n]})

and the obvious maps for
\mathsf{ft}
and
\partial
. We call this B-frame the
B-frame generated by a module
LM
over a monad
R
.
We write elements of
B_{n}
as
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n-1}\vdash A_{n}
,
and elements of
\widetilde{B}_{n+1}
as
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash t:A_{n+1}
, where
t\in RR({[n]})
.

More generally, the elements of
B_{n+1}
of a B-frame can be thought of as a pair of a context of length
n
, and a type in that context.
Hence, the elements of
B_{1}
are the types in the empty context.
Just like with C-systems, there is no explicit structure to denote types in a given context.
An element
t\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
is then a term, and the context and type
t
lives in is given by
\partial_{n+1}(t)
.

Example 4.3
.

Recall from
3.11
that
\mathsf{std}(n)
denotes the set
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
.
We shall consider the B-frame defined, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
by
B_{n}:=\{n\}
and
\tilde{B}_{n+1}:=\mathsf{std}(n)
.

Example 4.4
.

B-frames are the same as Garner’s “type-and-term structures”
[
Gar15
, Def. 8]
.
Garner
[
Gar15
, Prop. 13]
constructs an equivalence between the category of type-and-term structures
and the category of
\emptyset
-GATs, that is, of Generalized Algebraic Theories
[
Car86
]
without weakening, projection, and substitution rules, and interpretations between them.

We now define more structure on B-frames which represents operations on syntax.

The first operation could be called “slicing”; given a B-frame
\mathbb{B}
and a “context”
X\in B_{n}
in that B-frame, we construct the slice of
\mathbb{B}
over
X
:

Definition 4.5
.

For every B-frame
\mathbb{B}
and any
X\in B_{n}
, there is a B-frame

\mathbb{B}/X
given by

\displaystyle(B/X)_{m}

\displaystyle:=\{Y\in B_{n+m}\mid\mathsf{ft}^{m}(Y)=X\}

\displaystyle(\tilde{B}/X)_{m+1}

\displaystyle:=\{y\in\tilde{B}_{n+m+1}\mid\mathsf{ft}^{m+1}(\partial(y))=X\}.

Also, for any homomorphism
H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
of B-frames and any

X\in B_{n}
, there is a homomorphism
H/X:\mathbb{B}/X\to\mathbb{A}/H(X)

defined in the obvious way.

Note that for
X\in B_{n}
and
Y\in B_{n+m}
such that
\mathsf{ft}^{m}(Y)=X
,
we have an isomorphism
(\mathbb{B}/X)/Y\cong B/Y
of B-frames, constructed in the obvious way, which is natural in the sense that for any homomorphism
H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
of B-frames, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{B}/X)/Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}/Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}/H(X))/H(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/H(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H/X)/Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes.

Definition 4.6
.

Every B-frame
\mathbb{B}
has an underlying rooted tree given by the sets

B_{n}
and the functions
\mathsf{ft}_{n}\colon B_{n+1}\to B_{n}
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Similarly, a homomorphism of B-frames is in particular a homomorphism of rooted trees.
Thus we define

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Bfr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{RtTr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{R}}

to be the forgetful functor from B-frames to rooted trees.

We will now consider different type-theoretic structures on B-frames, specifically
substitution, weakening, and projection.
Garner considers similar structures in terms of algebras of suitable monads on the category of B-frames a. k. a. type-and-term structures.
We have not established a precise relationship (e.g., an equivalence) between our structures and the ones obtained by Garner as the algebras for “his” monads.

Definition 4.7
.

1.

A
substitution structure
on a B-frame
\mathbb{B}
is a collection of homomorphisms

S_{x}:\mathbb{B}/\partial(x)\to\mathbb{B}/\mathsf{ft}(\partial(x))

for all
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

A
weakening structure
on a B-frame
\mathbb{B}
is a collection of homomorphisms

W_{X}:\mathbb{B}/\mathsf{ft}(X)\to\mathbb{B}/X

for all
X\in B_{n+1}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

3.

The
structure of generic elements
on a B-frame
\mathbb{B}
equipped with weakening structure
W
is a collection of functions

\displaystyle\delta_{n}

\displaystyle:B_{n+1}\to\tilde{B}_{n+2}

such that
\partial(\delta_{n}(X))=W_{X}(X)
for any
X\in B_{n+1}
.

A
pre-B-system

\mathbb{B}
is a B-frame equipped with weakening structure, substitution structure, and generic elements.

Example 4.8
.

Consider the B-frame generated by a module
LM
over a monad
R
of
4.2
.
Given an element
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
, and hence in particular, a term
t\in R({[n]})
,
we obtain a substitution map
S_{x}:\mathbb{B}/\partial(x)\to\mathbb{B}/\mathsf{ft}(\partial(x))
that substitutes the term
t
for the “last” free variable in any element of
\mathbb{B}
lying “over”
\partial(x)
.
For instance, taking
x
to be
A_{0}\vdash t_{1}:A_{1}
, the substitution
S_{x}
maps the element
A_{0},A_{1}\vdash s:A_{2}
to
A_{0}\vdash s[t_{1}]:A_{2}[t_{1}]
.

For weakening, consider
X\in B_{1+1}
to be a context
A_{0}\vdash A_{1}
.
The weakening
W_{X}
maps any context of the form
A_{0},A^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,A^{\prime}_{n}\vdash A^{\prime}_{n+1}
to the weakened context
A_{0},A_{1},A^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,A^{\prime}_{n}\vdash A^{\prime}_{n+1}
, and similar for elements in
\widetilde{B}
.

For the generic element, consider, for instance, a context
X=A_{0}\vdash A_{1}
in
B_{2}
.
This context induces the generic element
A_{0},A_{1}\vdash\textsf{var}(1):A_{1}
, where
\eta(1)\in RR({[2]})
is the “de Bruijn” variable
1
bound by
A_{1}
in the context, and considered as a term by being wrapped in an application of the monadic unit
\eta
of the monad
RR
(the inclusion of variables into terms).
We have

\partial(A_{0},A_{1}\vdash\textsf{var}(1):A_{1})\kern 5.0pt=\kern 5.0ptA_{0},A_{1}\vdash A_{1}\kern 5.0pt=\kern 5.0ptW_{A_{0}\vdash A_{1}}(A_{0}\vdash A_{1}).

Example 4.9
.

Recall the B-frame of finite sets defined in
4.3
.
Here we construct structures of substitution, weakening and generic elements on it.

Note first that its slice on the (unique) element
n
in
B_{n}

is such that

(\tilde{B}/n)_{m+1}=\tilde{B}_{n+m+1}=\mathsf{std}(n+m).

It follows that a substitution structure
must consist of a family of functions

S_{x,j}\colon\mathsf{std}(n+1+j)\to\mathsf{std}(n+j)
,
for
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
.
We define
S_{x,j}:=s_{x}+\mathrm{id}_{j}
,
where
s_{x}
is the function
[\mathrm{id}_{n},x]\colon\mathsf{std}(n+1)\to\mathsf{std}(n)

given by the universal property of the coproduct
\mathsf{std}(n+1)
.
In other words,
S_{x,j}
lists all elements in
\mathsf{std}(n+j)

repeating the element
x\in\mathsf{std}(n)
in position
n+1
.
In particular, it fixes the first
n
elements,
and decreases the last
j
by
1
.

Similarly,
a weakening structure
must consist of a family of functions
W_{n,j}\colon\mathsf{std}(n+j)\to\mathsf{std}(n+1+j)
.
We define
W_{n,j}
to be the function
i_{n}+\mathrm{id}_{j}
,
where
i_{n}\colon\mathsf{std}(n)\to\mathsf{std}(n+1)
is the initial-segment inclusion.
In other words, it lists all elements in
\mathsf{std}(n+1+j)
except for
n
.
Equivalently, it fixes the first
n
elements,
and increases the remaining
j
by
1
.

Finally, the structure of generic elements is given by
an element
\delta_{n}\in\tilde{B}_{n+2}=\mathsf{std}(n+1)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
which we define to be its maximum,
that is,
\delta_{n}:=n
.

Taking advantage of the fact that finite sets are finite coproducts,
and slightly abusing notation,
we find it convenient to write

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}

for the functions

S_{x,j}\colon\mathsf{std}(n+1+j)\to\mathsf{std}(n+j)

and
W_{n,j}\colon\mathsf{std}(n+j)\to\mathsf{std}(n+1+j)
, respectively.

Example 4.10

(B-frames with structure and
D
-GATs)
.

Garner
[
Gar15
]
constructs an equivalence between the category of GATs and a category of algebras for a monad on B-frames.
We expect B-systems to be equivalent to Garner’s algebras.

Definition 4.11
.

1.

Consider two B-frames
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{A}
, both equipped with substitution structure. A homomorphism
H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
of B-frames is said to
preserve the substitution structure
if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}/\partial(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/\partial(H(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}/\mathsf{ft}(\partial(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/\mathsf{ft}(\partial(H(X)))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/\partial(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\tilde{H}(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/\mathsf{ft}(\partial(x))}

of B-frame homomorphisms commutes for every
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

Consider two B-frames
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{A}
, both equipped with weakening structure. A homomorphism
H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
of B-frames is said to
preserve the weakening structure
if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}/X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/H(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}/\mathsf{ft}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/\mathsf{ft}(H(X))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/\mathsf{ft}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{H(X)}}

of B-frame homomorphisms commutes for all
X\in B_{n}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

3.

Consider two B-frames
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{A}
, both equipped with weakening structure, and both equipped with generic elements. A B-frame homomorphism
H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
is said to
preserve the generic elements
if

\tilde{H}(\delta(X))=\delta(H(X))

for any
X\in B_{n+1}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

A
pre-B-homomorphism

H:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
is a homomorphism of pre-B-systems preserving the weakening structure, substitution structure and the generic elements.

Definition 4.12
.

A
B-system
is a pre-B-system for which the following conditions
hold:

1.

Every
S_{x}
is a pre-B-homomorphism.

2.

Every
W_{X}
is a pre-B-homomorphism.

3.

For every
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
one has
S_{x}\circ W_{\partial(x)}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{B}/\mathsf{ft}(\partial(x))}
.

4.

For every
x\in\tilde{B}_{n+1}
one has
S_{x}(\delta(\partial(x)))=x
.

5.

For every
X\in B_{n+1}
one has
S_{\delta(X)}\circ W_{X}/X=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{B}/X}
.

B-homomorphisms
are pre-B-homomorphisms between B-systems.
We denote the category of B-systems by
\mathbf{Bsys}
.

The idea is that first substitution and weakening preserve all the structure of a (pre-)B-system.
The third axiom asserts that substitution in weakened type families is constant.
Furthermore, the generic elements should behave like internal identity morphisms.
Axioms
4
and
5
are akin to two of the well-known monadic laws of substitution.

Lemma 4.13
.

The forgetful functor
\mathbf{Bsys}\to\mathbf{Bfr}
is faithful.

Proof.

This functor faithful because its action on morphisms only forgets a property.
∎

Example 4.14
.

The structures given in
4.9

make the B-frame defined in
4.3

into a B-system as follows.

Consider first homomorphism of B-frames

S_{y}\colon\mathbb{B}/(k+1)\to\mathbb{B}/k
,
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y\in\mathsf{std}(k)
.
The homomorphism
S_{y}
preserves the substitution structure if,
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathsf{std}(k+1+n)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+1+n+1+j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+n+1+j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+1+n+j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+n+j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y,n+1+j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{S_{y,n}(x),j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y,n+j}}

commutes.
This can be readily verified in the three cases

x<k
,
x=k
or
k<x<n+1+k
.
For example, in the last case
S_{y,n}(x)=x-1
and

The homomorphism
S_{y}

preserves the weakening structure if
for every
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+1+n+1+j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+n+1+j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+1+n+j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(k+n+j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y,n+1+j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{k+1+n,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y,n+j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{k+n,j}}

commutes. This is indeed the case:

\hbox to157.09pt{\vbox to126.93pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 78.54202pt\lower-63.46329pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-78.54202pt}{-57.85913pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Finally, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function

S_{y,n+1}\colon\mathsf{std}(k+1+n+1)\to\mathsf{std}(k+n+1)

preserves the maximum.
It follows that
S_{y}
preserves the generic elements.

We have shown that
S_{y}
is a pre-B-homomorphism.
We leave the verification that
W_{n}\colon\mathbb{B}/n\to\mathbb{B}/(n+1)

is a pre-B-homomorphism to the reader
and consider instead the remaining three conditions of
4.12
.

Condition
3
amounts to the commutativity of the left-hand diagram below, for every
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathsf{std}(n)
.
Its commutativity is shown in the right-hand diagram.

Condition
4
holds since

S_{x,0}(\delta_{n})=S_{x,0}(n)=x
,
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathsf{std}(n)
.

Condition
5
amounts to the commutativity of the left-hand diagram below, for every
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Its commutativity is shown in the right-hand diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{std}(n+1+j)}

Example 4.15

(B-systems and Generalized Algebraic Theories)
.

Continuing
4.10
, any Generalized Algebraic Theory (in Garner’s taxonomy also known as
\{w,p,s\}
-GATs) gives rise to a B-system.
The axioms of
4.12
follow mostly from the definition of substitution and the congruence rules that substitution satisfies.

Composing Cartmell’s equivalence of categories between contextual categories and GATs with our equivalence between B-systems and C-systems constructed in
Section

5.4
, we later can establish a more precise relationship between B-systems and GATs, in the form of an equivalence of categories.

4.2
The category of E-systems

In
Section

4.3
we will show how for any B-frame
we get a category
\mathcal{F}
with objects
(n,X)
where
X\in B_{n}
.
As we saw in
4.6
, the family of sets
B_{n}
induces a tree,
with objects
(n,X)
, and
\mathcal{F}
is the free category generated by this tree.
The sets
\tilde{B}_{n+1}
then induce a family of sets of terms indexed by the morphisms of
\mathcal{F}
.
In particular for a morphism
(n+1,X)\to(n,\mathsf{ft}(X))
we get a set of terms
\partial^{-1}(X)
.

In this section we will define the structure of a type theory directly on
\mathcal{F}
of the kind that one gets when turning a B-system into a category. Such systems are called E-systems, and in
Section

4.3
we will show that the category of B-systems is equivalent to a subcategory of E-systems. Thus, E-systems can be seen as a generalisation of B-systems.

Just like B-systems (and different from C-systems), E-systems have an explicit structure for “terms”.
Indeed, the first step towards the definition of E-system is that of a “term structure”:

Definition 4.16
.

A
category with term structure
is a category
\mathcal{F}
equipped with a family of sets
(T(A))_{A\in\mathrm{Mor}({\mathcal{F}})}
indexed by the morphisms
\mathrm{Mor}({\mathcal{F}})
of
\mathcal{F}
. Given two categories
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{D}
with term structure, a
functor with term structure
from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{D}
is a functor
F:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
equipped with a family of functions
T(A)\to T(F(A))
for every morphism
A
in
\mathcal{F}
.

Any B-frame, and hence any B-system, generates a category with term structure;
details will be given in
4.62
.

The identity functor with term structure
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
is the identity functor on
\mathcal{F}
equipped with the identity functions
T(A)\to T(A)
indexed by the morphisms
A
in
\mathcal{F}
. Similarly, the composition
G\circ F
of two functors
F
and
G
with term structure is defined to be the composition of the underlying functors, equipped with the composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(F(A))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(G(F(A))).}

Definition 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a strict category with term structure and
\Gamma
an object of
\mathcal{F}
.
The
slice term structure
on the strict slice category
\mathcal{F}/\Gamma

is given by
T_{\mathcal{F}/\Gamma}(A)=T_{\mathcal{F}}(A)
.

Remark 4.18
.

Every functor
F:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
with term structure
gives rise to a functor with term structure
F/X:\mathcal{F}/X\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}/F(X)
.

In order to illustrate the additional structure
that we shall consider on a category with term structure,
we introduce the following example.

Example 4.19
.

Consider the the poset
(\mathbb{N},\geq)
.
We write
(n,k)\colon n+k\geq n
for arrows in
(\mathbb{N},\geq)
.
Let
\mathcal{N}
be the category with term structure
which consists of the poset
(\mathbb{N},\geq)
and
the term structure given by
T(n,k):=\mathbf{Set}([k],[n])
,
i.e. the set of functions from the standard set with
k
elements
to the standard set with
n
elements.

4.2.1
Substitution systems

Given a (strict) category
\mathcal{F}
, an object
\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}
and an object
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
, we will write
{\Gamma}.{A}
for the domain of
A
. In other words,
A
is a morphism
{\Gamma}.{A}\to\Gamma
.

Definition 4.20
.

A
pre-substitution structure
on a strict category with term structure
\mathcal{F}
consists
of a functor with term structure
S_{x}:\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}\to\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
for every
x\in T(A)
and

A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
, such that
S_{x}(\mathrm{id}_{{\Gamma}.{A}})=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}
.

A
pre-substitution system
is a strict category with term structure together with a
pre-substitution structure.

Definition 4.21
.

A
pre-substitution homomorphism

F:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
is a functor with term structure for
which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}/F({\Gamma}.{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}/F(\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/{\Gamma}.{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{F(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/\Gamma}

commutes for every
x\in T(A)
and
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
.

Definition 4.22
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a pre-substitution system and
\Gamma
an object of
\mathcal{F}
.
The
slice pre-substitution structure

on the strict slice category with term structure
\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
from
4.17

is given by
S(\mathcal{F}/\Gamma)_{x}=S(\mathcal{F})_{x}
,
for every
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
,
P\in\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}

and
x\in T_{\mathcal{F}}(P)
.

Definition 4.23
.

A
substitution system
is a pre-substitution system for which each

S_{x}

is a pre-substitution homomorphism. A
substitution homomorphism
is a
pre-substitution homomorphism between substitution systems.

Corollary 4.24
.

For any object
\Gamma
of a substitution system, the strict slice category
\mathcal{F}/\Gamma

is a substitution system.

Remark 4.25
.

The condition that every
S_{x}
is a substitution homomorphism, asserts that
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{{\Gamma}.{A}}.{P}}.{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{\Gamma}.{S_{x}(P)}}.{S_{x}(Q)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{\Gamma}.{A}}.{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{S_{x}(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}/{P}.{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{S_{x}(y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}/P}

commutes for every
y\in T(Q)
.

Example 4.26
.

We can equip the category with term structure
\mathcal{N}
from
4.19

with a substitution structure as follows.
Consider the functor
-k\colon\mathbb{N}/(n+k)\to\mathbb{N}/n

that maps
(n+k+j,l)
to
(n+j,l)
.
It preserves terminal objects
since an arrow
(m,i)
is an identity if and only if
i=0
.
Given
(n,k)\colon n+k\geq n
and a function
f\colon[k]\to[n]
,
define
S_{f}\colon\mathcal{N}/(n+k)\to\mathcal{N}/n
as the functor
-k

together with functions
T(n+k+j,l)\to T(n+j,l)
defined by postcomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+k+j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+k+j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+j]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{S_{f}(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\mathrm{id}_{n},f]+\mathrm{id}_{j}}

where
[\mathrm{id}_{n},f]
is the function given by the universal property of the coproduct
[n]\leftarrow[n+k]\to[k]
in
\mathbf{Set}
,
and similarly for
[\mathrm{id}_{n},f]+\mathrm{id}_{j}
.

The fact that
S_{f}
is a pre-substitution homomorphism follows from the fact
that postcomposition distributes on
[-,-]
as shown below:
given
g\colon[l]\to[n+k+j]
, then

S_{f}/(n+k,j)\circ S_{g}=S_{S_{f}(g)}\circ S_{f}/(n+k,j+l)

since

\displaystyle\left([\mathrm{id}_{n},f]+\mathrm{id}_{j}\right)[\mathrm{id}_{n+k+j},g]

\displaystyle=[[\mathrm{id}_{n},f]+\mathrm{id}_{j},S_{f}(g)]

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{id}_{n+j},S_{f}(g)]\left([\mathrm{id}_{n},f]+\mathrm{id}_{j+l}\right).

Example 4.27
.

Consider a group
G
. A term structure on
G
consists of a set
T(g)
for every element
g
of
G
.

A pre-substitution structure on
G
consists of a functor with term structure
S_{x}:G/\bullet\to G/\bullet
(where
\bullet
denotes the only object in
G
viewed as a category) for every
g\in G
and every
x\in T(g)
such that
S_{x}(\mathrm{id}_{\bullet})=\mathrm{id}_{\bullet}
. One can show that such functors
S_{x}:G/\bullet\to G/\bullet
correspond to functions
G\to G
which preserve the identity, so a pre-substitution structure amounts to functions
S_{x}:G\to G
for every
g\in G
,
x\in T(g)
preserving the identity together with functions
S_{x}:T(h)\to T(S_{x}(h))
for every
g,h\in G
,
x\in T(g)
.

A substitution structure
T
on
G
is a pre-substitution structure
S
as described above such that the following diagrams commute for all
g,h,k\in G
,
x\in T(g)
, and
y\in T(h)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{S_{x}(y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(S_{x}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(S_{y}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(S_{x}S_{y}k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{S_{x}(y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{x}}

Now for a particular example, suppose that each
T(g)
is
\mathrm{Aut}(G)
, that each
S_{x}:G\to G
is just the automorphism
x
, and that each
S_{x}:T(h)\to T(S_{x}h)
takes
y\in T(h)
to
xyx^{-1}
. Then we find indeed that the first diagram commutes since
(xyx^{-1})x=xy
for all
x\in S_{x}=\mathrm{Aut}(G)
and all
y\in S_{y}=\mathrm{Aut}(G)
. The second diagram commutes since
(xyx^{-1})xzx^{-1}(xyx^{-1})^{-1}=xyzy^{-1}x^{-1}
for all
x\in T(g)=\mathrm{Aut}(G)
,
y\in T(h)=\mathrm{Aut}(G)
, and
z\in T(k)=\mathrm{Aut}(G)
.

4.2.2
Weakening systems

Definition 4.28
.

Consider a category
\mathcal{F}
with term structure
T
.
A
pre-weakening structure
on
\mathcal{F}
is a family of functors with term structure
W_{A}:\mathcal{F}/\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}
indexed by the morphisms
A:{\Gamma}.{A}\to\Gamma
in
\mathcal{F}
such
that

1.

W_{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}/\Gamma}
for every object
\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}
.

2.

W_{A\circ P}=W_{P}\circ W_{A}
for every
P\in\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}
and
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
.

3.

W_{A}
strictly preserves the final object, i.e.,
W_{A}(\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma})=\mathrm{id}_{{\Gamma}.{A}}
.

A
pre-weakening system
is a strict category with term structure equipped with a pre-weakening structure.

Definition 4.29
.

A
pre-weakening homomorphism

F:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
between pre-weakening systems
is a functor
F:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{D}
with term structure such that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}/F({\Gamma}.{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}/F(\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/{\Gamma}.{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F/\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{F(A)}}

of functors with term structure commutes for any
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
.

Definition 4.30
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a pre-weakening system and
\Gamma
an object of
\mathcal{F}
.
The
slice pre-weakening system
on the strict slice category with term structure from
4.17

\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
is given by
W(\mathcal{F}/\Gamma)_{P}=W(\mathcal{F})_{P}
for every
P\in\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}
and
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
.

Definition 4.31
.

A
weakening system
is a pre-weakening system
\mathcal{F}
such that
W_{A}
is a pre-weakening homomorphism for every morphism
A
in
\mathcal{F}
. A
weakening homomorphism
is a pre-weakening
homomorphism between weakening systems.

Remark 4.32
.

The condition that every
W_{A}
is a pre-weakening homomorphism implies that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{\Gamma}.{B}}.{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{\Gamma}.{A}}.{W_{A}({B}.{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{\Gamma}.{A}}.{W_{A}(B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{A}/{B}.{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{A}/B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{W_{A}(Q)}}

commutes for each
A,B\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
and
Q\in\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{B}
. On objects, this
property asserts that for any
k\in\mathcal{F}/\mathrm{E}
, the dotted arrows in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma}.{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma}.{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{A}/{B}.{Q})(W_{Q}(R))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{(W_{A}/B)(Q)}((W_{A}/B)(R))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{Q}(R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{A}/B)(Q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{A}/B)(R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{A}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

are equal.

A useful special case of this property is where
B=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}
. Thus, if
W
is
a weakening system, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{{\Gamma}.{A}}.{W_{A}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{A}/C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{W_{A}(C)}}

commutes for every
A,C\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
. In particular, we see
that
W_{A}(W_{C}(D))=W_{W_{A}(C)}(W_{A}(D))
for any
D\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
,
i.e. that weakening is a self-distributive operation.

Corollary 4.33
.

For any object
\Gamma
of a weakening system
\mathcal{F}
, the strict slice category
\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
is a weakening system.

Example 4.34
.

Consider the term category
\mathcal{N}
from
4.19
.
We can equip
\mathcal{N}
with a weakening structure as follows.
Consider the functor
+k\colon\mathbb{N}/n\to\mathbb{N}/(n+k)

that maps
(n+j,l)
to
(n+k+j,l)
.
It preserves terminal objects as in
4.26
.
Given
(n,k)\colon n+k\geq n
,
define
W_{n,k}\colon\mathcal{N}/n\to\mathcal{N}/(n+k)
as the functor
+k

together with functions
T(n+j,l)\to T(n+k+j,l)
defined by postcomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+k+j]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{W_{n,k}(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}^{n+k}+\mathrm{id}_{j}}

where
i_{n}^{n+k}\colon[n]\to[n+k]
is the initial-segment inclusion and

i_{n}^{n+k}+\mathrm{id}_{j}
is the function given by the universal property of the coproduct
[n]\leftarrow[n+j]\to[j]
in
\mathbf{Set}
.

The fact that
W_{n,k}
is a pre-weakening homomorphism follows from
the fact that initial-segment inclusions factor uniquely into inclusions whose images have codimension 1:
given
(n+j,l)
in
\mathcal{N}/n
, then

W_{n,k}/(n,j+l)\circ W_{n+j,l}=W_{W_{n,k}(n+j,l)}\circ W_{n,k}/(n,j)

since

(i_{n}^{n+k}+\mathrm{id}_{j+l})i_{n+j}^{n+j+l}=i_{n+k+j}^{n+k+j+l}(i_{n}^{n+k}+\mathrm{id}_{j}).

Example 4.35
.

Consider the situation of
4.27
above where the underlying category is a group
G
with term structure
S
.

A pre-weakening structure on
G
