Definition 5.24
.

Let
{f}\in\mathrm{thom}(A,B)
and
F\in\mathrm{thom}_{f}(P,Q)
. Then we
define the E-homomorphism

F^{\bullet}:=F^{\ast}\circ(f^{\ast}/Q):\mathbb{E}/{{\Gamma}{B}}.{Q}\to\mathbb{E}/{{\Gamma}{A}}.{P}.

The infix notation of
F^{\bullet}
is taken to be
-\bullet F
.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
{f}\in\mathrm{thom}(A,B)
and
F\in\mathrm{thom}_{f}(P,Q)
. Then we
have the equality

F^{\bullet}=(f\ltimes F)^{\ast}.

Proof.

\displaystyle F^{\ast}\circ(f^{\ast}/Q)

\displaystyle=S_{F}\circ W_{P}\circ S_{f}/(W_{A}(Q))\circ W_{A}/{B}.{Q}

\displaystyle=S_{F}\circ S_{W_{P}(f)}/W_{P}(W_{A}(Q))\circ W_{P}/W_{A}({B}.{Q})\circ W_{A}/{B}.{Q}

\displaystyle=S_{F}\circ S_{W_{P}(f)}/W_{P}(W_{A}(Q))\circ W_{{A}.{P}}/{B}.{Q}

\displaystyle=S_{{W_{P}(f)}.{F}}\circ W_{{A}.{P}}/{B}.{Q}

(By
4.55
)

\displaystyle=(f\ltimes F)^{\ast}.\qed

In the next theorem we prove the interchange law of horizontal and vertical composition.
Its proof uses the following fact.

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{thom}(A,B)
be an internal morphism in context
\Gamma
. Then one has

f^{\ast}\circ W_{B}=W_{A}.

Proof.

The proof is a simple calculation:

f^{\ast}\circ W_{B}=S_{f}\circ W_{A}/B\circ W_{B}=S_{f}\circ W_{W_{A}(B)}\circ W_{A}=W_{A}.\qed

Theorem 5.27
.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in context
\Gamma
. Then the equality

{{(g\ltimes G)}\circ{(f\ltimes F)}}={({g}\circ{f})\ltimes(G\bullet F)}

of morphisms from
{A}.{P}
to
{C}.{R}
in context
\Gamma
holds.

Proof.

By
5.25
, we have

\displaystyle{(g\ltimes G)}\circ{(f\ltimes F)}

\displaystyle={{({\langle{Q}\rangle{g}}.{G})}\circ{f}}\circ{F}

(By
5.25
)

\displaystyle={{(\langle{{Q}\circ{f}}\rangle{{g}\circ{f}})}.{({G}\circ{f})}}\circ{F}

\displaystyle={({\langle{{Q}\circ{f}}\rangle{{g}\circ{f}}}\circ{F})}.{({{G}\circ{f}}\circ{F})}

\displaystyle={({\langle{{Q}\circ{f}}\rangle{{g}\circ{f}}}\circ{F})}.{(G\bullet F)}

\displaystyle={(\langle{P}\rangle{{g}\circ{f}})}.{(G\bullet F)}

(By
5.26
)

\displaystyle=({g}\circ{f})\ltimes(G\bullet F).\qed

Theorem 5.28
.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in context
\Gamma
.
Then
F^{\bullet}\circ G^{\bullet}=(G\bullet F)^{\bullet}
.
In other words the composition
-\bullet-
is associative.

Proof.

\displaystyle F^{\bullet}\circ G^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=(f\ltimes F)^{\ast}\circ(g\ltimes G)^{\ast}

(By
5.25
)

\displaystyle=({g\ltimes G}\circ{f\ltimes F})^{\ast}

(By
5.16
)

\displaystyle=({g}\circ{f}\ltimes G\bullet F)^{\ast}

(By
5.27
)

\displaystyle=(G\bullet F)^{\bullet}.

(By
5.25
)

∎

Theorem 5.29
.

Let
{f}\in\mathrm{thom}(A,B)
and
F\in\mathrm{thom}_{f}(P,Q)
. Then

f\ltimes F
is the unique morphism from
{A}.{P}
to
{B}.{Q}

with the property that both the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}.{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}.{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\ltimes F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes and
{\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{B,Q}}\circ{f\ltimes F}=F
.

Proof.

We first note that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,Q}}\circ{f\ltimes F}

\displaystyle={{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,Q}}\circ{f}}\circ{F}

(By
5.25
)

\displaystyle={\langle{{Q}\circ{f}}\rangle{{\mathsf{idtm}_{B}}\circ{f}}}\circ{F}

\displaystyle={\langle{{Q}\circ{f}}\rangle{f}}\circ{F}

\displaystyle=\langle{P}\rangle{f}

(By
5.26
)

\displaystyle={f}\circ{{\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,P}\right)}}.

(By
5.19
)

Also, we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{B,Q}}\circ{f\ltimes F}

\displaystyle={{\mathsf{idtm}_{Q}}\circ{f}}\circ{F}

(By
5.25
)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{idtm}_{{Q}\circ{f}}}\circ{F}

\displaystyle=F.

Thus, we conclude that
f\ltimes F
has indeed the stated property. For
the uniqueness, let
G:{A}.{P}\to{B}.{Q}
be a morphism such that

{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,Q}}\circ{G}={f}\circ{{\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,P}\right)}}
and

{\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{B,Q}}\circ{G}=F
. Then it follows that

G={{f}\circ{{\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,P}\right)}}}.{F}={\langle{P}\rangle{f}}.{F}=f\ltimes F.\qed

5.2.3
The functor from E-systems to CE-systems

Let
{\mathbf{Esys}}_{*}
be the category of pointed E-systems:
objects are pairs
(\mathbb{E},\Gamma)
of an E-systems
\mathbb{E}
and an object
\Gamma
in its underlying strict category,
and arrows are E-homomorphisms that preserve the distinguished object.
There is an evident forgetful functor
{\mathbf{Esys}}_{*}\to\mathbf{Esys}

together with an embedding
\mathbf{E2E_{*}}\colon\mathbf{Esys}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{Esys}}_{*}

which picks out the terminal object of an E-system.

Problem 5.30
.

To construct a functor
\mathbf{E_{*}2CE}\colon{\mathbf{Esys}}_{*}\to\mathbf{CEsys}
.

Construction 5.31

(for
5.30
)
.

Let
(\mathbb{E},\Gamma)
be a pointed E-system and
consider the category of terms
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
from
5.17
.
Define a functor
I_{\mathbb{E}}^{\Gamma}\colon\mathcal{F}/\Gamma\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
as follows.
It is the identity on objects and maps an arrow

Q\colon{A}.{Q}\to A
in
\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
to
\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,Q}\in\mathrm{thom}({A}.{Q},A)
.
For functoriality, we compute

\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,\mathrm{id}_{{\Gamma}.{A}}}=\langle{\mathrm{id}_{{\Gamma}.{A}}}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{A}}=\mathsf{idtm}_{A}
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,Q.R}

\displaystyle=\langle{Q.R}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{A}}=\langle{R}\rangle{(\langle{Q}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{A}})}=\langle{R}\rangle{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,Q}}

\displaystyle={\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,Q}\right)}^{*}\!{\left(\langle{R}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{Q}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,Q}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{Q,R}.

Given
f\in\mathrm{thom}(A,B)
and
R
in
\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{B}
,
there is
{R}\circ{f}
in
\mathcal{F}/{\Gamma}.{A}
.
We define

\pi_{2}(f,R):=f\ltimes\mathsf{idtm}_{{R}\circ{f}}\colon{A}.{{R}\circ{f}}\to{B}.{R}.

(39)

Then the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}.{{R}\circ{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}.{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(f,R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,{R}\circ{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(40)

commutes.
The functoriality conditions follow immediately from the interchange
laws proven in
Section

5.2.2
.

To show that (
40
) is a pullback square,
consider a morphism
g:X\to A
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
.
Then we have the isomorphisms

\{h\in\mathrm{thom}(X,{B}.{Q})\mid{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,Q}}\circ{h}={f}\circ{g}\}\cong T(({f}\circ{g})^{\ast}(Q))\cong\{u\in\mathrm{thom}(X,{A}.{{Q}\circ{f}})\mid{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,{Q}\circ{f}}}\circ{u}=g\}

Thus, we find for every
h:X\to{B}.{Q}
satisfying

{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{B,Q}}\circ{h}={f}\circ{g}
, a unique morphism

u:X\to{A}.{{Q}\circ{f}}
satisfying

{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,{Q}\circ{f}}}\circ{u}=g
. It is easy to verify
that
{\pi_{2}(f,Q)}\circ{u}=h
, so the universal property of pullbacks holds.

Let now
(\mathbb{D},\Delta)
be a pointed E-system and let

F\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{D}
be an E-homomorphism such that
F\Gamma=\Delta
.
In particular, for every
A,B\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/\Gamma

there is a function
F\colon T(\langle{A}\rangle{B})\to T(\langle{FA}\rangle{FB})
.
These functions give the action on arrows of a functor

F_{\Gamma}\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}}\!\left(F\Gamma\right)

whose action on objects is given by

F/\Gamma\colon\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/\Gamma\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{D}}/F\Gamma
.
Functoriality of
F_{\Gamma}
follows from the fact that
F
is a projection homomorphism and
5.11
.
Using
4.58
, we see that

F_{\Gamma}\circ I_{\mathbb{E}}^{\Gamma}=I_{\mathbb{D}}^{F\Gamma}\circ(F/\Gamma)
.
Finally, it follows from
5.11
and
5.22

that
F_{\Gamma}
preserves the choice of pullback squares.

We have described the action of
\mathbf{E_{*}2CE}
on objects and arrows.
Its functoriality is straightforward.
∎

We obtain a functor
\mathbf{E2CE}\colon\mathbf{Esys}\to\mathbf{CEsys}
defining

\mathbf{E2CE}:=\mathbf{E_{*}2CE}\circ\mathbf{E2E_{*}}
.

Remark 5.32
.

For every E-system
\mathbb{E}
and every
\Gamma
,
the CE-system
\mathbf{E_{*}2CE}(\mathbb{E},\Gamma)
is rooted.
The canonical terminal object

\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/\Gamma
is terminal in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)

by
5.18
since for every
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/\Gamma

\mathrm{thom}(A,\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma})=T(W_{A}(\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}))=T(\mathrm{id}_{{\Gamma}.{A}}).

Next we give the choice of pullbacks in a CE-system in the image of
\mathbf{E2CE}

in terms of the underlying E-system structure.

Lemma 5.33
.

For
\mathbb{E}
an E-sytem and
\Gamma
an object in
\mathbb{E}
,
consider the CE-system
\mathbb{A}:=\mathbf{E_{*}2CE}(\mathbb{E},\Gamma)
.
For every
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{}}/\Gamma
and
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{}}/{\Gamma}.{A}
it is

{\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,P}\right)}^{*}\!{Q}=\langle{P}\rangle{Q}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{}}/{{\Gamma}.{A}}.{P}

and

\pi_{2}(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{A,P},Q)=\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{P,\langle{P}\rangle{Q}}\in\mathrm{thom}({P}.{\langle{P}\rangle{Q}},Q).

Proof.

The first equality follows from
5.19
.
For the second one:

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma},A},B)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma},A}\ltimes\mathsf{idtm}_{\langle{A}\rangle{B}}

\displaystyle={{\left(W_{\langle{A}\rangle{B}}\langle{A}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}}}\right)}}.{\mathsf{idtm}_{\langle{A}\rangle{B}}}

\displaystyle={{\left(W_{A}\langle{B}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}}}\right)}}.{{\left(W_{A}\mathsf{idtm}_{B}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\langle{A}\rangle{({\langle{B}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}}}}.{\mathsf{idtm}_{B}})}

\displaystyle=\langle{A}\rangle{{\left({\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma},B}}.{\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma},B}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\langle{A}\rangle{\mathsf{idtm}_{B}}=\mathsf{idtm}_{\langle{A}\rangle{B}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{A,\langle{A}\rangle{B}}.\qed

5.3
Equivalence between E-systems and CE-systems

In this section, we show that the functors constructed in
Sections

5.2
and
5.1
form an adjunction that, when suitably restricted, yields an equivalence of categories between rooted CE-systems and E-systems.

Specifically, we prove the following results:

Theorem 5.34
.

1.

The functor
\mathbf{E2CE}
is left adjoint to
\mathbf{CE2E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CEsys}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Esys}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CE2E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E2CE}}

2.

The functor
\mathbf{E2CE}
is full and faithful.

3.

The image of
\mathbf{E2CE}
is equivalent to
\mathbf{rCEsys}
.

Consider the functors
\mathbf{E2rCE}
and
\mathbf{rCE2E}
obtained obtained by
(co)restricting
\mathbf{E2CE}
and
\mathbf{CE2E}
to
\mathbf{rCEsys}
.

Corollary 5.35
.

The pair of adjoint functors
\mathbf{E2CE}
and
\mathbf{CE2E}
induces an equivalence
between the category
\mathbf{Esys}
of E-systems
and the category
\mathbf{rCEsys}
of rooted CE-systems.

Proof.

The equivalence follows from
5.34
.
3
and
the fact that a coreflective subcategory is equivalent to its image.
∎

To prove
5.34
we construct unit and counit and prove the triangular identities.
In this proof we denote as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

(41)

the canonical isomorphism of strict categories,
for any strict category
\mathcal{F}
with a terminal object
1
.
We may still leave this isomorphism implicit when doing so creates no confusion.

Problem 5.36
.

To construct, for each E-system
\mathbb{E}
, an E-homomorphism
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{CE2E}\circ\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})
,
naturally in
\mathbb{E}
.

Construction 5.37

(for
5.36
)
.

Let
\mathbb{E}
be an E-system and denote its terminal object by
[]
.
In this proof we shall decorate with a hat the constituents
of the E-system structure of

\hat{\mathbb{E}}:=\mathbf{CE2E}\circ\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})
.
The underlying pre-category of
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
is
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/[]
and,
for
X\in(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/[])/!_{\Gamma}

\hat{T}(X)=\left\{h\in\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Gamma},!_{{\Gamma}.{X}})\mid{\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{!_{\Gamma},!_{{\Gamma}.{X}}}}\circ{h}=\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}\right\}.

We define
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}
as the functor
!\colon\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/[]
in (
41
)
with term structure given by the bijections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{T}(!(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

(42)

from
5.18
,
that is, for
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/\Gamma
and
t\in T(A)
,
it is
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}(t):={\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{t}
.

To conclude that this defines an E-homomorphism we compute
for
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}/\Gamma

\displaystyle\hat{W}_{!(A)}\circ(!/\Gamma)

\displaystyle={\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{!_{\Gamma},A}\right)}^{*}\!{\,}\circ(!/\Gamma)=W_{A}\circ(!/\Gamma)

\displaystyle=(!/{\Gamma}.{A})\circ W_{A},

and for
t\in T(A)
,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{!(t)}\circ(!/{\Gamma}.{A}))

\displaystyle={\left({\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{t}\right)}^{*}\!{\,}

\displaystyle=S_{{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{t}}\circ(W_{!_{\Gamma}}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})

\displaystyle=S_{t}\circ(S_{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}\circ(W_{!_{\Gamma}}/!_{\Gamma}))/A

\displaystyle=(!/\Gamma)\circ S_{t},

and finally

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathsf{idtm}_{A})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{\mathsf{idtm}_{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{{\Gamma}.{A}}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathsf{idtm}}_{!(A)}.

Naturality in
\mathbb{E}
requires that any
E-homomorphism
F\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{D}

commutes with
\eta
as functors, this is clear,
with term structures, and this follows from

5.11
and
4.58
.
∎

Problem 5.38
.

To construct, for each CE-system
\mathbb{A}
, a CE-homomorphism
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbf{E2CE}\circ\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{A}
,
naturally in
\mathbb{A}
.

Construction 5.39

(for
5.38
)
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a CE-system and let
\mathbb{E}:=\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})

be the associated E-system.
The underlying functor of the CE-system

\hat{\mathbb{A}}:=\mathbf{E2CE}\circ\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})

is
I_{\mathbb{E}}\colon\mathcal{F}/[]\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}

defined in
5.31
. As before, we decorate with a hat the constituents of
the CE-system structure of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
.
For
\Gamma,\Delta
in
\mathcal{F}
,
recall that

\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma})=\{\Delta\overset{x}{\longrightarrow}{\Delta}.{({!_{\Delta}}^{*}\!{!_{\Gamma}})}\mid I({!_{\Delta}}^{*}\!{!_{\Gamma}})\circ x=\mathrm{id}_{\Delta}\}

and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\Delta,\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

(43)

be the function that maps
x
to the arrow

\pi_{2}\left(I(!_{\Delta}),!_{\Gamma}\right)\circ x
of
\mathcal{C}
.
The functions
\psi
give rise to a functor

\Psi\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathcal{C}

as follows.
It maps identities to identities since
the identity on
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}

is the only
h\in\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma})

such that
\pi_{2}\left(I(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma}\right)\circ h=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}
.
To see that it preserves composites,
consider the commutative diagram below which defines the composite
{y}\circ{x}
of

x\in\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma})
and
y\in\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Xi})

in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{({!_{\Delta}}^{*}\!{!_{\Gamma}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma}.{({!_{\Gamma}}^{*}\!{!_{\Xi}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{({!_{\Delta}}^{*}\!{!_{\Xi}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}\left(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(!_{\Gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}\left(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Xi}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}\left(!_{\Delta},!_{\Xi}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y}\circ{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(!_{\Xi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(!_{\Gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(!_{\Delta})}

Functoriality of
\Psi
amounts to the commutativity of the upper face.

To conclude that
(\mathrm{d},\Psi)
is a CE-homomorphism
it remains to show that it preserves chosen pullbacks,
since the square below commutes by definition of
\Psi
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}/[]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathbb{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Let then
x\in\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma})
and
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
.
It is

\displaystyle{x}^{\hat{*}}\!{\left(!(A)\right)}

\displaystyle=S_{x}\circ(W_{!_{\Delta}}/!_{\Gamma})\circ!(A)

\displaystyle={x}^{*}\!{\,}\circ({I(!_{\Delta})}^{*}\!{\,}/!_{\Gamma})\circ!(A)

\displaystyle=!\left({\left(\pi_{2}\left(I(!_{\Delta}),!_{\Gamma}\right)\circ x\right)}^{*}\!{A}\right)

\displaystyle=!\left({\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}\right)

whereas

\Psi(\hat{\pi_{2}}\left(x,!(A)\right))=\pi_{2}(I(!_{\Delta.({\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A})}),!_{\Gamma.A})\circ\hat{\pi_{2}}\left(x,!(A)\right)=\pi_{2}\left(\Psi(x),A\right)

holds by commutativity of the upper face in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma}.{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{idtm}_{{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(\Psi(x),A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\pi_{2}}(x,!(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(I(!_{\Delta}),!_{\Gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi(x)}^{*}\!{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\Delta}}

This diagram commutes because all the squares not involving the top-left object are chosen pullback squares in
\mathbb{A}
,
two of the remaining triangles commute by definition of
\mathsf{idtm}
,
and the third one involving
\hat{\pi_{2}}(x,!(A))
commutes by (
39
) and
5.20
.

The component
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathbf{E2CE}\circ\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{A}

of the counit at
\mathbb{A}
is defined to be the pair
(\mathrm{d},\Psi)
.
To see that this choice is natural in
\mathbb{A}
it is enough to show that the square of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{B})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathbb{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\mathbb{B}}}

commutes for every CE-homomorphism
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
.
Note that the action of the left-hand functor coincide with that of
F
.
Commutativity of the square thus follows from

F(\pi_{2}\left(x,A\right))=\pi_{2}\left(Fx,FA\right)

which holds by definition of CE-homomorphism.
∎

Lemma 5.40
.

1.

For every E-system
\mathbb{E}
,
the E-homomorphism
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}
from
5.36
is invertible.

2.

For every CE-system
\mathbb{A}
,
the CE-homomorphism
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}
from
5.38
is invertible
if and only if
\mathbb{A}
is rooted.

Proof.

1
.
By construction, the E-homomorphism
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}
is invertible as a functor with term structure.
The inverse is an E-homomorphism too:

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})\circ\hat{W}_{!(A)}

\displaystyle={\left(\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{!_{\Gamma},A}\right)}^{*}\!{\,}\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{\Gamma})=S_{W_{A}(\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}})}\circ(W_{!_{{\Gamma}.{A}}}/!_{\Gamma})\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=W_{A}\circ S_{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}\circ(W_{!_{\Gamma}}/!_{\Gamma})\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=W_{A}\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{\Gamma}),

and for
t\in T(A)
,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}/!_{\Gamma})\circ\hat{S}_{!(t)}

\displaystyle={\left({\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{t}\right)}^{*}\!{\,}\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})=S_{{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{t}}\circ(W_{!_{\Gamma}}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})

\displaystyle=S_{t}\circ(S_{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}\circ(W_{!_{\Gamma}}/!_{\Gamma}))/A\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})

\displaystyle=S_{t}\circ(\mathrm{d}/!_{{\Gamma}.{A}}),

and finally

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\hat{\mathsf{idtm}}_{!(A)})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!_{{\Gamma}.{A}},W_{A}(A)}}[{{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{{\Gamma}.{A}}}}.{{\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!_{\Gamma},A}}[{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{{\Gamma}.{A}}}}]}}]

\displaystyle={\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!_{\Gamma},A}}[{{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{\mathsf{idtm}_{A}}}]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{idtm}_{A}.

2
.
Note first that each function
\psi
in (
43
) induces a bijection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{f\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta,\Gamma)\mid I(!_{\Gamma})\circ f=I(!_{\Delta})\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(44)

with inverse given by the universal property of the canonical pullback square below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta}.{{!_{\Delta}}^{*}\!{!_{\Gamma}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(!_{\Gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(!_{\Delta})}

As soon as
\mathsf{1}
is terminal in
\mathcal{C}
,
the right-hand set in (
44
)
coincide with
\mathcal{C}(\Delta,\Gamma)
.
Conversely, if the counit components are invertible it follows
from (
44
) that

\mathcal{C}(\Delta,\mathsf{1})=\{!_{\Delta}\}
.
∎

Proof of
5.34
.

1
.
To complete the proof we show that,
for an E-system
\mathbb{E}
and a CE-system
\mathbb{A}

\mathbf{CE2E}(\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}})\circ\eta_{\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})}\circ\mathbf{E2CE}(\eta_{\mathbb{E}})=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})}.

It is clear that these equations hold between functors on families
by the isomorphism in (
41
).
It remains to show that they hold also between the term structures in the left-hand one,
and between functors on substitutions in the right-hand one.

For a CE-system
\mathbb{A}
, a family
A\in\mathcal{F}/\Gamma
and

y\in T(A)=\{x\colon\Gamma\to{\Gamma}.{A}\mid I(A)\circ x=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma}\}
,

5.33
yields

\eta_{\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})}(y)=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}},{\pi_{2}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma})}^{*}\!{A})\circ y
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{CE2E}(\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}})\circ\eta_{\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})}(y)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(!_{\Gamma},!_{{\Gamma}.{A}})\circ\pi_{2}(\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}},{\pi_{2}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma})}^{*}\!{A})\circ y

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma}),A)\circ\pi_{2}(\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}},{\pi_{2}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma})}^{*}\!{A})\circ y

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}(!_{\Gamma},!_{\Gamma})\circ\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}},A)\circ y

\displaystyle=y.

For an E-system
\mathbb{E}
, objects
\Delta
and
\Gamma
and
f\in\mathrm{thom}(!_{\Delta},!_{\Gamma})
,

5.33
and
4.59
yield

\varepsilon_{\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})}\circ\mathbf{E2CE}(\eta_{\mathbb{E}})(f)={\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!_{\Delta},\langle{!_{\Delta}}\rangle{!_{\Gamma}}}}[{{\mathsf{idtm}_{!_{\Gamma}}}.{f}}]=f.

This concludes the proof of the adjunction.

2
.
It is a consequence of
5.40
.
1
.

3
.
We have already observed in
5.32
that, for every E-system
\mathbb{E}
, the CE-system
\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})
is rooted.
The converse follows from
5.40
.
2
.
∎

5.4
Equivalence between B-systems and C-systems

Here we describe the main contribution of our work:
the construction of an equivalence of categories between the category of
C-systems of
Section

3
and the category of B-systems of
Section

4
.

Lemma 5.41
.

The functor
\mathbf{CE2E}\colon\mathbf{CEsys}\to\mathbf{Esys}
restricts to a functor

\mathbf{CE2E}\colon\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}\to\mathbf{\mathbf{Esys}_{s}}
between stratified systems.

Proof.

To see that the E-system
\mathbf{CE2E}(\mathbb{A})
is stratified
whenever the rooted CE-system
\mathbb{A}
is stratified,
note first that the underlying category
\mathcal{F}
is stratified by assumption.
Weakening and substitution homomorphisms are stratified
since the pullback functor that defines them in

5.6
.(
37
,
38
)
is stratified.

For a stratified CE-homomorphism
F
, the underlying functor of
the E-homomorphism
\mathbf{CE2E}(F)
is the component
F_{\mathcal{F}}
of
F

on families, which is stratified by assumption.
∎

Lemma 5.42
.

The functor
\mathbf{E2CE}\colon\mathbf{Esys}\to\mathbf{rCEsys}
restricts to a functor

\mathbf{E2CE}\colon\mathbf{\mathbf{Esys}_{s}}\to\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}
between stratified systems.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{E}
be a stratified E-system.
In particular, the underlying category
\mathcal{F}
is stratified.
Since weakening and substitution homomorphisms are also stratified
by assumption,
so is the precomposition homomorphisms from
5.10
.
It follows that the CE-system
\mathbf{E2CE}(\mathbb{E})
is stratified.

For a stratified E-homomorphism
F
,
the component on families of the CE-homomorphism
\mathbf{E2CE}(F)

is the underlying functor of
F
,
which is stratified by assumption.
∎

Lemma 5.43
.

1.

For every stratified E-system
\mathbb{E}
,
the unit component
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}
of
5.37

is a stratified E-homomorphism.

2.

For every stratified CE-system
\mathbb{A}
,
the counit component
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}
of
5.39

is a stratified CE-homomorphism.

3.

The adjunction
\mathbf{E2CE}\dashv\mathbf{CE2E}
from
5.34
.
1

restricts to an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathbf{CEsys}_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathbf{Esys}_{s}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CE2E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E2CE}}

between subcategories of stratified structures.

Proof.

1.

The underlying functor of the unit component
\eta_{\mathbb{E}}
is
the functor
!\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}/[]
from (
41
).
This functor is stratified since
L([])=0
.

2.

The underlying functor of the counit component
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}

on families is the inverse
\mathrm{d}\colon\mathcal{F}/\mathsf{1}\to\mathcal{F}

of
!\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}/\mathsf{1}
,
and it is stratified for the same reason.

3.

This is a consequence of
5.41
and
5.42

and
Items

1
and
2
just proved.

∎

Define a functor
\mathbf{C2B}\colon\mathbf{Csys}\to\mathbf{Bsys}
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Csys}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathbf{Esys}_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Bsys}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CE}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CE2E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E2B}}

(45)

where the functors are, in order,

\mathbf{CE}
from
3.24

\mathbf{CE2E}
from
5.6

and
\mathbf{E2B}
from
4.80
.
Similarly, we obtain a functor
\mathbf{B2C}\colon\mathbf{Bsys}\to\mathbf{Csys}

in the other direction as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Bsys}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathbf{Esys}_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\mathbf{rCEsys}_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Csys}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B2E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E2CE}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}

(46)

where the functors are, in order,

\mathbf{B2E}
from
4.71
,

\mathbf{E2CE}
from
5.31

and
\mathbf{C}
from
3.28
.

Theorem 5.44
.

The pair of functors
\mathbf{C2B}
and
\mathbf{B2C}

establish an equivalence between the category of C-systems
and the category of B-systems.

Proof.

The functors defining
\mathbf{C2B}
in (
45
) and
\mathbf{B2C}
in (
46
)
are essentially inverse to each other
by
3.30
,
5.35
and
4.83
.
The claim follows since equivalences compose.
∎

6
Conclusion

We have constructed an equivalence between the category of C-systems and the category of B-systems, each equipped with a suitable notion of morphism.
The equivalence does not rely on classical reasoning principles such as the axiom of choice or excluded middle.
This equivalence constitutes a crucial piece in Voevodsky’s research program on the formulation and solution of an initiality conjecture.

Some questions that remain open:

•

Voevodsky has studied different type constructions on C-systems, in particular, dependent function types
[
Voe16a
,
Voe17b
]
and identity types
[
Voe15b
]
.
The equivalence constructed in the present paper should be extended to type and term constructors on C-systems and B-systems.

•

Via Generalized Algebraic Theories, B-systems and C-systems relate to Garner’s algebras for a monad on type-and-term systems
[
Gar15
]
, in the form of an equivalence of categories.
It would be very useful to have an explicit description of the maps back and forth, without passing through GATs.

•

E-systems and CE-systems should be related to other unstratified categorical structures for the interpretation of type theory, such as categories with families
[
Dyb96
]
.
